# [AvaTaR]{#14.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval {#avatar-optimizing-llm-agents-for-tool-assisted-knowledge-retrieval .ltx_title .ltx_title_document}

::: ltx_abstract
###### Abstract {#abstract .ltx_title .ltx_title_abstract}

Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated impressive capability in utilizing external tools and knowledge to boost accuracy and reduce hallucinations. However, developing the prompting techniques that make LLM agents able to effectively use external tools and knowledge is a heuristic and laborious task. Here, we introduce [AvaTaR]{#23.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, a novel and automatic framework that optimizes an LLM agent to effectively use the provided tools and improve its performance on a given task/domain. During optimization, we design a comparator module to iteratively provide insightful and holistic prompts to the LLM agent via reasoning between positive and negative examples sampled from training data. We demonstrate [AvaTaR]{#23.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} on four complex multimodal retrieval datasets featuring textual, visual, and relational information. We find [AvaTaR]{#23.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} consistently outperforms state-of-the-art approaches across all four challenging tasks and exhibits strong generalization ability when applied to novel cases, achieving an average relative improvement of 14% on the Hit@1 metric. Code and dataset are available at [[https://github.com/zou-group/avatar]{#23.1.4.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter style="color:#00FFFF;"}.](https://github.com/zou-group/avatar){.ltx_ref .ltx_href target="_blank"} [^†^[[^†^[footnotetext: ]{.ltx_note_type}Correspondence: [{shirwu, jure, jamesz}@cs.stanford.edu]{#footnotex1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnotex1 .ltx_note .ltx_role_footnotetext}
:::

:::::::::: {#S1 .section .ltx_section}
## [1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Introduction {#introduction .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S1.p1 .ltx_para}
Autonomous agents powered by large language models (LLMs) offer substantial promise for complex problem-solving \[[37](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}, [48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}, [56](#bib.bib56){.ltx_ref}, [35](#bib.bib35){.ltx_ref}, [7](#bib.bib7){.ltx_ref}\]. These agents demonstrate remarkable capabilities in reasoning \[[42](#bib.bib42){.ltx_ref}, [41](#bib.bib41){.ltx_ref}, [48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}, [47](#bib.bib47){.ltx_ref}\] and planning \[[13](#bib.bib13){.ltx_ref}, [53](#bib.bib53){.ltx_ref}, [14](#bib.bib14){.ltx_ref}, [8](#bib.bib8){.ltx_ref}\]. Moreover, their functionality is extended by employing external tools that provide access to external/private data and specialized operations, such as APIs for interacting with knowledge bases and search engines. These tools empower agents to perform complex tasks like multi-step problem-solving and retrieving diverse information, which is essential for complex question-answering and retrieval \[[36](#bib.bib36){.ltx_ref}, [34](#bib.bib34){.ltx_ref}, [43](#bib.bib43){.ltx_ref}, [22](#bib.bib22){.ltx_ref}, [13](#bib.bib13){.ltx_ref}, [19](#bib.bib19){.ltx_ref}, [29](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}\].
:::

::: {#S1.p2 .ltx_para}
Despite the promising capabilities of LLM agents, it remains challenging to engineer effective prompts that guide these agents through a multi-stage procedure for real-world problem-solving. This procedure includes (1) decomposing a complex question into an actionable plan with simpler steps, (2) strategically utilizing provided tools to gather relevant information, and, finally, (3) synthesize the intermediate results to produce a coherent and accurate response. Each step requires extensive manual effort and numerous iterations of trial and error to perfect the prompts.
:::

::: {#S1.p3 .ltx_para}
Current approaches have primarily focused on directly deploying agents using complex human-designed ["mega-prompts"]{#S1.p3.1.1 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} \[[20](#bib.bib20){.ltx_ref}, [48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}, [16](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}\], which requires lots of manual trial-and-error. Nevertheless, such hand-engineered mega-prompts may also result in brittle implementations with suboptimal accuracy (see Figure [[2]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} (a)), where the ReAct agent \[[48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}\] [easily produces trivial and misleading answers to customers' queries about specific products.]{#S1.p3.1.2 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} Furthermore, existing research \[[45](#bib.bib45){.ltx_ref}, [40](#bib.bib40){.ltx_ref}, [5](#bib.bib5){.ltx_ref}, [6](#bib.bib6){.ltx_ref}, [55](#bib.bib55){.ltx_ref}, [49](#bib.bib49){.ltx_ref}, [52](#bib.bib52){.ltx_ref}\] on employing LLMs as optimizers often fails to adequately refine the complex strategies for enhancing tool integration and usage. This lack of strategic optimization can lead to less effective, non-generalizable agent applications in complex real-world scenarios.
:::

::: {#S1.p4 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Present work: [AvaTaR]{#S1.p4.1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}]{#S1.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. To address these challenges, we introduce [AvaTaR]{#S1.p4.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, an automated framework that optimizes Agents for effective Tool utilization and excellent task performance. [We demonstrate our framework on challenging and common tasks of knowledge base Retrieval, which involves the complex multi-stage procedure and extensive tool usage.]{#S1.p4.1.3 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} [To highlight, we leverage the key insights from contrastive learning and build a comparator module ("trainer") to generate holistic instructions/prompts ([i.e., ]{#S1.p4.1.4.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}compute robust "gradient") to optimize an actor LLM. ]{#S1.p4.1.4 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} Specifically, [AvaTaR]{#S1.p4.1.5 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} includes two phases:
:::

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/x1.png" id="S1.F1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="438" height="214" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span> <span id="S1.F1.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Overview of <span id="S1.F1.4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span>.</span> <span id="S1.F1.5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> consists of an actor LLM and a comparator LLM. (a) During optimization, the actor generates actions to answer queries leveraging the provided tools. Then the comparator contrasts a set of well-performing (positive) and poorly-performing (negative) queries, and automatically generates holistic prompts to teach the actor more effective retrieval strategies and tool usage (<span id="S1.F1.6.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">cf. </span>Section <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref" title="4 Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>). (b) At deployment, the optimized actor can be effectively utilized to answer new queries.</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S1.p5 .ltx_para}
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i1}

  ::: {#S1.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
  [Optimization phase]{#S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. The core of our optimization framework (Figure [[1]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) is a comparator LLM that automatically generates holistic prompts to teach an actor LLM to differentiate between best-performing and poor-performing tool usage. [The comparator takes positive and negative data samples on which the current agent performs well and poorly, respectively, to identify the overall gap and systematic errors exhibited by the agent. Unlike per-sample instructions which easily lead to overfitting on individual data point, by constructing multiple samples as a "batch", comparator can extract a more robust "gradient" to "backpropagate" to the actor. In other words, the comparator can provide more effective and adaptive prompts through such batch-wise contrastive reasoning, promoting the agent to identify flaws in solving the challenging multi-stage problem. ]{#S1.I1.i1.p1.1.2 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} Following previous methods \[[37](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}, [26](#bib.bib26){.ltx_ref}, [54](#bib.bib54){.ltx_ref}, [49](#bib.bib49){.ltx_ref}\], we also maintain a memory bank with selected past instructions to prevent the actor LLM from repeating previous mistakes.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i2}

  ::: {#S1.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
  [Deployment phase]{#S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. The iterative optimization through our [AvaTaR]{#S1.I1.i2.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} framework updates actor for more effective and generalizable knowledge extraction, allowing direct [generalization]{#S1.I1.i2.p1.1.3 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} to novel user inquiries in the deployment time. In Figure [[2]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} (b), the optimized actor creates three novel strategies 1) the precise decomposition of problems via extracting multifaceted attributes, 2) effective tool usage via a sophisticated and robust scoring system, and 3) The strategic combination of different scores, determined by the learned coefficients, ensures accurate and comprehensive retrieval.
  :::
:::

::: {#S1.p6 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Experimental evaluation]{#S1.p6.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. We conduct extensive experiments on four retrieval datasets. The retrieval tasks are of high complexity with multimodal data, including textual, visual, and relational information. [AvaTaR[ consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, showing a substantial 14% improvement in the Hit@1 metric. Impressively, with only 25 iterations, ]{#S1.p6.1.2.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_upright}AvaTaR[ boosts the Hit@1 metric from an initial 5.1% to 28.6% on ]{#S1.p6.1.2.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_upright}Flickr30K-Entities[ \[[31](#bib.bib31){.ltx_ref}\] and the Recall@20 metric from 30.3% to 39.3% on ]{#S1.p6.1.2.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_upright}STaRK-Prime[ \[[44](#bib.bib44){.ltx_ref}\]. These improvements, achieved through iterative updates to the prompts, underscore ]{#S1.p6.1.2.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_upright}AvaTaR[ 's ability to optimize agents for complex task and tool usage.]{#S1.p6.1.2.5 .ltx_text .ltx_font_upright}]{#S1.p6.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps style="color:#000000;"}
:::

::: {#S1.p7 .ltx_para}
Our key contributions are:

- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i1}

  ::: {#S1.I2.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  We introduce [AvaTaR]{#S1.I2.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, a novel framework that optimizes an actor for effective tool utilization through a comparator module that automatically generate holistic prompts.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i2}

  ::: {#S1.I2.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  We demonstrate that [AvaTaR]{#S1.I2.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} on four challenging retrieval tasks, which significantly outperforms existing agent methods in terms of the task performance and generalization ability.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I2.i3}

  ::: {#S1.I2.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
  We provide a comprehensive analysis on the actor's evolution during optimization, highlighting how comparator automatically provides targeted instructions that improve and generalize the actor.
  :::
:::

<figure id="S1.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/x2.png" id="S1.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_portrait" width="461" height="583" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2: </span><span id="S1.F2.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Comparison between <span id="S1.F2.3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> and ReAct</span>. (a) The ReAct agent exhibits incomplete task decomposition and employs suboptimal tool combinations like lengthy string matching, leading to poor task performance. (b) <span id="S1.F2.4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> decomposes the task into multiple steps such as type filtering and flexible token matching. Moreover, it implements robust tool usage and precise synthesis with learned parameters from the optimization phase to achieve excellent performance on new queries.</figcaption>
</figure>
::::::::::

:::::: {#S2 .section .ltx_section}
## [2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}[Related Work]{#S2.1.1 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} {#related-work .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S2.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[LLM Agents]{#S2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Recent research has been leveraging the remarkable language understanding and reasoning abilities of LLMs \[[42](#bib.bib42){.ltx_ref}, [47](#bib.bib47){.ltx_ref}, [2](#bib.bib2){.ltx_ref}, [37](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}, [48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}\] to complete downstream tasks. For complex tasks that require more capabilities, previous works regard LLMs as agents that can interact with the environments \[[7](#bib.bib7){.ltx_ref}, [36](#bib.bib36){.ltx_ref}, [22](#bib.bib22){.ltx_ref}, [43](#bib.bib43){.ltx_ref}, [13](#bib.bib13){.ltx_ref}, [19](#bib.bib19){.ltx_ref}, [16](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}, [48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}, [5](#bib.bib5){.ltx_ref}, [23](#bib.bib23){.ltx_ref}\] and leverage external tools \[[35](#bib.bib35){.ltx_ref}, [29](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}, [57](#bib.bib57){.ltx_ref}, [34](#bib.bib34){.ltx_ref}, [58](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref}, [7](#bib.bib7){.ltx_ref}, [27](#bib.bib27){.ltx_ref}, [32](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref}, [24](#bib.bib24){.ltx_ref}\]. For example, ReAct \[[48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}\] conducts reasoning and action in an interleaved way, which retrieves information from Wikipedia to support reasoning.
:::

::: {#S2.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[LLM Agents for Retrieval]{#S2.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Previous research has applied LLM agents for Information Retrieval (IR) systems, through pretraining \[[9](#bib.bib9){.ltx_ref}, [50](#bib.bib50){.ltx_ref}, [3](#bib.bib3){.ltx_ref}, [15](#bib.bib15){.ltx_ref}\], reranking \[[12](#bib.bib12){.ltx_ref}, [38](#bib.bib38){.ltx_ref}\], and prompting techniques \[[16](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}, [11](#bib.bib11){.ltx_ref}\]. In the IR systems, retriever module directly influences the performance of downstream tasks, such as retrieval-augmented generation \[[18](#bib.bib18){.ltx_ref}, [25](#bib.bib25){.ltx_ref}, [26](#bib.bib26){.ltx_ref}\] and knowledge-intensive question answering \[[30](#bib.bib30){.ltx_ref}, [46](#bib.bib46){.ltx_ref}\]. For example, EHRAgent \[[36](#bib.bib36){.ltx_ref}\] is designed for EHR question-answering, capable of retrieving relevant clinical knowledge through a structured tool-use planning process and an interactive coding mechanism. However, these LLM agents usually take heuristic (zero-shot) prompts or rely on few-shot examples \[[21](#bib.bib21){.ltx_ref}, [48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}, [16](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}, [36](#bib.bib36){.ltx_ref}\] to apply to downstream tasks, which lack more informed guidance on generating effective retrieval strategies and tool-assisted actions.
:::

::: {#S2.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Agent Optimization]{#S2.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. In the field of improving LLM agents, previous works have modified the parameters of LLM backbones through finetuning or instruction tuning to enhance agent capability \[[29](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}, [51](#bib.bib51){.ltx_ref}, [33](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref}, [4](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref}, [17](#bib.bib17){.ltx_ref}, [28](#bib.bib28){.ltx_ref}\] or generated better prompts through iterative prompt tuning \[[45](#bib.bib45){.ltx_ref}, [49](#bib.bib49){.ltx_ref}, [40](#bib.bib40){.ltx_ref}, [16](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}, [11](#bib.bib11){.ltx_ref}\]. Recently, Zhang et al[.]{.ltx_text} \[[52](#bib.bib52){.ltx_ref}\] conducted agent training by iteratively updating the agents' functions according to the execution history. However, these methods do not explicitly consider targeted optimization on tool usage and the impact on complex multi-stage tasks. Moreover, improving the generalization ability of agents \[[10](#bib.bib10){.ltx_ref}, [39](#bib.bib39){.ltx_ref}, [27](#bib.bib27){.ltx_ref}\], which is essential for real-world applications, has received less attention. In our work, we focus on automatically generating holistic instructions via a novel contrastive reasoning mechanism, targeting effective tool usage and agents' generalization ability.
:::
::::::

::::::: {#S3 .section .ltx_section}
## [3 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Problem Formulation {#problem-formulation .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S3.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Definition 1: Tools]{#S3.p1.4.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. We define tools or APIs as a set of implemented functions with specified input and output variables. We denote the abstract tool space as $\mathcal{T} = {\{ f_{k}:{{\mathcal{I}_{f_{k}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}_{f_{k}} \mid k} = 1},{2,\ldots}}\}}$, where $f_{k}$ maps the input $\mathcal{I}_{f_{k}}$ to the output $\mathcal{O}_{f_{k}}$. For example, the tools can be APIs used for accessing external knowledge given a search index, an encoder model that generates vector representations from text or image data, or a task-specific classifier that outputs probabilities over a list of classes.
:::

::: {#S3.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Definition 2: Agents]{#S3.p2.5.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. An LLM agent, defined as $\mathcal{A}:{\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\alpha}$, which is controlled by verbal prompts to generate a flow of actions needed to complete a task. Here $\alpha$ denotes the action sequence $\lbrack\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{L}\rbrack$, where each action is defined by a tuple $({{f \in \mathcal{T}},{{i \in \mathcal{I}_{f}},{o \in \mathcal{O}_{f}}}})$ consisting of a tool function, specified input(s), and a designated variable that receives the output(s). Each action in the sequence can leverage the outputs generated by previous actions. The final action $\alpha_{L}$ renders the results for the task.
:::

::: {#S3.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Multi-step problem-solving]{#S3.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Real-world problems are inherently complex and cannot be effectively addressed with straightforward solution or simple tool usage alone. Using LLM agents for solving real-world problems can be structured into a multi-stage procedure:

- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I1.i1}

  ::: {#S3.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  Decomposition of the problem: The procedure begins by breaking down a complex question into an actionable plan characterized by simpler steps. This decomposition is crucial for setting clear objectives and facilitating focused problem-solving.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I1.i2}

  ::: {#S3.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  Tool-assisted subproblem solving: In the subsequent phase, agents strategically utilize tools from the established tool space $\mathcal{T}$ to gather solutions for each step. This stage is essential for acquiring the necessary information required to address each subproblem of the decomposed problem effectively.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S3.I1.i3}

  ::: {#S3.I1.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
  Synthesis and response formulation: The final stage involves synthesizing the intermediate results to construct a precise response. This synthesis not only combines the data but may also refine the response through trials and adjustments, ensuring the solution's accuracy and relevancy.
  :::
:::

::: {#S3.p4 .ltx_para}
For example, retrieval tasks are inherently complex and demanding. [Given a user query $q$, retrieval tasks are to identity or generate a rank of relevant entities $E$ from the entity space of a knowledge base. Each query is associated with a set of ground truth answers, denoted as $Y$, which are used to compute the quality of the prediction.]{#S3.p4.3.3 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} Specifically, the LLM agent is required to 1) comprehend a user's request, followed by 2) using the provided tools to identify and analyze relevant information in the large knowledge space, which may contain multi-modal data source. Finally, it requires 3) the integration of all gathered information to reason and generate an accurate response.
:::
:::::::

::::::::::::::::::: {#S4 .section .ltx_section}
## [4 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}[Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks]{#S4.1.1 .ltx_text style="color:#000000;"} {#our-method-optimizing-agents-for-tool-assisted-multi-step-tasks .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

<td rowspan="2" id="S4.T1.5.1.1.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.1.3.1" class="ltx_text">Memory</span></td>
<td rowspan="2" id="S4.T1.5.1.1.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.1.4.1" class="ltx_text">Generalization</span></td>

<td id="S4.T1.5.1.2.1" class="ltx_td"></td>

</tr>
<tr id="S4.T1.5.1.3" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.3.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_t">ReAct [<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>]</td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.3.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.3.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.3.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.3.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.3.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.3.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.3.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.3.5.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="S4.T1.5.1.4" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.4.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left">Self-refine [<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>]</td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.4.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.4.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.4.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.4.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.4.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.4.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.4.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.4.5.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="S4.T1.5.1.5" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.5.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left">Reflexion [<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>]</td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.5.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.5.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.5.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.5.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.5.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.5.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.5.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.5.5.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#FF0000;">✗</span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="S4.T1.5.1.6" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.6.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> (Ours)</td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.6.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.6.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.6.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.6.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.6.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.6.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
<td id="S4.T1.5.1.6.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"><span id="S4.T1.5.1.6.5.1" class="ltx_text" style="color:#80FF00;">✔</span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 1: </span><span id="S4.T1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Key differences between <span id="S4.T1.3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> and prevailing agent methods.</span> <span id="S4.T1.4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> demonstrates the capabilities to: 1) self-improve on specific tasks, 2) retain memory throughout the optimization process, 3) enhance the agent’s ability to generalize, and 4) autonomously generate holistic, high-quality prompts for better tool usage. Please refer to Section <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref" title="4 Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> for details.</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S4.p1 .ltx_para}
Each step in the multi-stage problem-solving process (formulated in Section [[3]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S3 "3 Problem Formulation ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) requires effective prompts to identify key flaws and improve task performance. However, perfecting the agents' prompts requires extensive manual effort and numerous iterations of trial and error.
:::

::: {#S4.p2 .ltx_para}
Therefore, we offer an automatic and novel optimization framework, [AvaTaR]{#S4.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, to generate prompts to improve agents' tool usage and task performance. In Table [[1]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 4 Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, we highlight four critical aspects of our approach compared with the prevailing agent frameworks \[[48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}, [37](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}, [23](#bib.bib23){.ltx_ref}\]. Here we introduce two main LLM components in [AvaTaR]{#S4.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, an actor LLM (Section [[4.1]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Actor Construction and Challenges ‣ 4 Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) and a comparator LLM (Section [[4.2]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Automate Holistic Instruction Generation with Comparator ‣ 4 Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}).
:::

::::: {#S4.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [4.1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Actor Construction and Challenges {#actor-construction-and-challenges .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS1.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Actor]{#S4.SS1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. The actor agent follows the definition in Section [[3]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S3 "3 Problem Formulation ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, which is responsible for generating initial actions given the initial instructions/prompts and modifying actions given other instructions. Specifically, the initial instructions provide details about the task and the existing tools, where the tools can be introduced in programming languages such as Python. During optimization, the prompts further incorporate the previous action sequence and updated instructions to modify these actions. The actor then generates revised actions, which could be a blend of tool usage through the programming language (code generation), along with natural language explanations of how the tools are used.
:::

::: {#S4.SS1.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Challenges on multi-step complex tasks]{#S4.SS1.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. A common approach to updating instructions uses execution results or performance data from a specific instance, often through techniques like self-explanation \[[23](#bib.bib23){.ltx_ref}, [5](#bib.bib5){.ltx_ref}\] or self-reflection \[[37](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}, [49](#bib.bib49){.ltx_ref}\]. However, this may not be suitable for complex tasks involving tool usage. As complex multi-step tasks include several interacting factors that influence overall performance, such as problem decomposition and tool selections, these per-sample instructions tend to be narrow and fail to identify flaws across all components of a complex solution. Additionally, while certain tool combinations may be effective for one type of input, their effectiveness can vary with others, leading to decreased performance when applied across different scenarios.
:::
:::::

:::::::: {#S4.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [4.2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Automate Holistic Instruction Generation with Comparator {#automate-holistic-instruction-generation-with-comparator .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
To address the challenges, we construct a comparator LLM to update the instructions for the actor. Instead of optimizing on a sampled instance, comparator aims to identify systematic flaws throughout the structured actions/solutions.
:::

::: {#S4.SS2.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Step 1: Constructing positive and negative queries]{#S4.SS2.p2.6.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. To realize this goal, in Figure [[1]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, the comparator samples a group of data samples (question-answer pairs), executes the current actions for each question, and constructs well-performing (positive) and poorly-performing (negative) queries based on their execution results. Specifically, we define two thresholds, $\ell$ and $h$ (where $0 < h \leq \ell < 1$), as the lower and upper bounds for constructing positive and negative queries, respectively. Queries with an evaluation metric ([e.g., ]{#S4.SS2.p2.6.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}Recall) value greater than $\ell$ are classified as positive, while those with a value less than $h$ are classified as negative. The two thresholds control the distinction between these two query groups. In each iteration, we sample the positive and negative mini-batches with an equal number of samples, with the total batch size $b$ as a hyperparameter.
:::

::: {#S4.SS2.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Step 2: Generating instruction by contrastive reasoning]{#S4.SS2.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. After that, the comparator is asked to contrast the two groups of queries based on their key characteristics, attribute the performance gap to particular tool usage in the complex solution, and finally suggest general modifications that can lead to overall improvement on task performance. The instructions generated by the comparator will then be appended to the initial prompts to update the actor.
:::

::: {#S4.SS2.p4 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Insights/Justification for the comparator]{#S4.SS2.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. To illustrate the insights, we draw an analogy from deep neural network training, where extremely small batch sizes can introduce significant noise in gradient estimates and high variance in model updates. By adopting a batched training strategy and sampling positive and negative queries as two "mini-batches", comparator is able to extract a robust "gradient" to update the actor. This approach motivates comparator to generate more general and comprehensive instructions on the complex action sequence, including problem decomposition, solutions to subproblems, and the final synthesis. Moreover, as contrastive reasoning directly targets disentangling the performance gap related to input patterns and how they are handled differently by the tools, it is particularly effective in helping comparator differentiate and select tools for use. Finally, by identifying systemic flaws across a wide array of negative queries, comparator generates modifications that are not only tailored to individual samples but also to diverse data samples, offering benefits for better generalization to novel samples.
:::

<figure id="S4.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/x3.png" id="S4.F3.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="461" height="248" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3: </span><span id="S4.F3.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Demonstration example during optimization.</span> Best viewed in color. The task of the comparator is to automatically generate instructions based on sampled positive and negative queries. Then comparator provides holistic instructions that guide the actor to improve query decomposition, utilize better tools, and incorporate more comprehensive information.</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S4.SS2.p5 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Demonstration example]{#S4.SS2.p5.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Figure [[3]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4.2 Automate Holistic Instruction Generation with Comparator ‣ 4 Our Method: Optimizing Agents for Tool-Assisted Multi-Step Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} shows an example where comparator contrasts the patterns of positive and negative queries, identifying discrepancies in tool usage within the action sequence. It reveals that compared to positive queries, negative queries feature more complex product descriptions, more subtle brand mentions, and additional relevant product mentions. These observations suggest: 1) an incomplete problem decomposition involving query attributes like detailed product features, 2) a potentially imprecise brand match using embedding similarity, and 3) a lack of consideration for related products in the results. Informed by these insights, actor updates its action sequence to include the additional sbuproblems and use the tools more effectively for the task, such as replacing the embedding tool with an LLM verification tool.
:::
::::::::

::::::: {#S4.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [4.3 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Logistic Instructions and Memory Construction {#logistic-instructions-and-memory-construction .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S4.SS3.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Logistic instructions]{#S4.SS3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. While instructions from the comparator are designed to improve task performance, we incorporate two types of orthogonal instructions to ensure the actions are valid and can be executed efficiently.
:::

::: {#S4.SS3.p2 .ltx_para}
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S4.I1.i1}

  ::: {#S4.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  Validity check: This instruction is triggered internally during the execution of each action. It ensures the validity of the actor's actions, such as verifying the proper use of function calls.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S4.I1.i2}

  ::: {#S4.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  Timeout Error: To preclude inefficient action sequences that may stall the actor, we implement a timeout mechanism that triggers an error when processing exceeds a threshold. This error prompt the actor to use more efficient strategies, such as eliminating redundant operations.
  :::
:::

::: {#S4.SS3.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Memory Bank]{#S4.SS3.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. During optimization, we utilize a memory bank inspired by human decision-making processes, where humans typically address current problems by analyzing the current situation and referencing past experiences. The memory bank stores tuples of action sequences, instructions from comparator, and the performance of these action sequences on a small QA set (conducted during sampling positive and negative queries). To manage the context size input to actor , we take only the top-$5$ action sequences with the best performance. This memory bank enables actor to learn from both immediate instructions and historical results.
:::

::: {#S4.SS3.p4 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Deployment]{#S4.SS3.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. At deployment (see Figure [[1]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}), we apply the optimized actor /action sequence, which includes effective tool usage and problem-solving strategies, to directly answer user queries.
:::
:::::::
:::::::::::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::: {#S5 .section .ltx_section}
## [5 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Experiments {#experiments .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S5.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Tasks and Evaluation]{#S5.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. We conduct experiments on four challenging retrieval datasets (Appendix [[A]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A1 "Appendix A Retrieval Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) from [STaRK]{#S5.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} \[[44](#bib.bib44){.ltx_ref}\] and [Flickr30K-Entities]{#S5.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} \[[31](#bib.bib31){.ltx_ref}\] to demonstrate [AvaTaR]{#S5.p1.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} in handling complex real-world task. For each query, the task is to retrieve relevant entities such as nodes in the knowledge graph or images in the knowledge bases. We assess task performance by comparing the consistency of the results with the ground truth answers in the datasets, where we use Hit@1, Hit@5, Recall@20, and Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) as the metrics.
:::

::: {#S5.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Baselines]{#S5.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Following Wu et al[.]{.ltx_text} \[[44](#bib.bib44){.ltx_ref}\], we employ several embedding-based retriever models for our evaluation: Vector Similarity Search (VSS) and Multi-Vector Similarity Search (Multi-VSS) using [text-embedding-ada-002]{#S5.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} from OpenAI; a relation-aware model, QAGNN \[[50](#bib.bib50){.ltx_ref}\], for the [STaRK]{#S5.p2.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} benchmark. Moreover, we include two prevailing agent frameworks to further enrich our evaluation.

- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S5.I1.i1}

  ::: {#S5.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
  [ReAct]{#S5.I1.i1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} \[[48](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref}\] conducts reasoning and action in an in-context and interleaved manner to enhance LLMs with the capability to interactively analyze observed information and perform actions.
  :::
- [[•]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S5.I1.i2}

  ::: {#S5.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
  [Reflexion]{#S5.I1.i2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} \[[37](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}\] utilizes self-reflection on the current task completion and store these reflections in an episodic memory buffer to enhance decision-making in subsequent trials.
  :::
:::

::: {#S5.p3 .ltx_para}
We also include an ablation model, [AvaTaR]{#S5.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}-C, which removes the comparator in our optimization pipeline. By default, we use Claude 3 Opus as the backbone LLM for all agent approaches in the main paper and report part of results using GPT-4 Turbo (0125) in Appendix [[B]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2 "Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}.
:::

::: {#S5.p4 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Function library]{#S5.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Our function library (Appendix [[D]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A4 "Appendix D Function library ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) consists of twenty-eight functions that facilitate access to, operation on, and reasoning over the knowledge information by LLM agents. We used the same function library for ReAct, Reflexion, and [AvaTaR]{#S5.p4.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} agents.
:::

::: {#S5.p5 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[General pipeline]{#S5.p5.2.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. For [AvaTaR]{#S5.p5.2.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, we leverage the same implementation, including the same structure of the initial prompts, the metric Recall@20 for constructing positive and negative queries, and hyperparameters ($\ell = h = 0.5$, $b = 20$), for all four datasets. Initially developed for [STaRK-Amazon ]{#S5.p5.2.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, this pipeline was then directly applied to the other datasets.
:::

:::::::: {#S5.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [5.1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Textual and Relational Retrieval Tasks {#textual-and-relational-retrieval-tasks .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S5.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
We employ the [Amazon]{#S5.SS1.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, [MAG]{#S5.SS1.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, and [Prime]{#S5.SS1.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} datasets from the [STaRK]{#S5.SS1.p1.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} benchmark \[[44](#bib.bib44){.ltx_ref}\], a large-scale semi-structured retrieval benchmark that integrates textual and relational knowledge ([cf. ]{#S5.SS1.p1.1.5 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}detailed description in Appendix [[A]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A1 "Appendix A Retrieval Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}). Here, the entities to be retrieved are defined as nodes in a graph structure, the knowledge associated with each entity includes both textual descriptions and relational data about the entities. We used the official splits in [STaRK]{#S5.SS1.p1.1.6 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} benchmark.
:::

::: {#S5.SS1.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Takeaway 1: [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p2.1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} outperforms the state-of-the-art models]{#S5.SS1.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Table [[2]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 5.1 Textual and Relational Retrieval Tasks ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} shows that [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} substantially outperforms leading models such as Reflexion across all metrics on the [STaRK]{#S5.SS1.p2.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} benchmark. To highlight, the average improvement of [AvaTaR[ ]{#S5.SS1.p2.1.4.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}]{#S5.SS1.p2.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} is 15.6% on Hit@1 and 9.5% on MRR. ReAct agents, however, can not optimized from instructions on better tool usage and tend to select tools based on the LLM's prior knowledge, which may not be optimal for the given task. We observe that ReAct agents applied highly similar tools across various queries and are hard to "jump out of the box" for better tool usage even with extensive in-context reasoning. In Appendix [[B]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2 "Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, we provide results of the agent methods using GPT-4 Turbo, which has similar conclusions discussed here.
:::

::: {#S5.SS1.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Takeaway 2: Comparator greatly impacts the actor's performance]{#S5.SS1.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. The comparison of [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p3.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} with its ablation variant, [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p3.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}-C, highlights the significant advantages of the comparator module. Although [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p3.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}-C conducts validity and timeout checks, integrating Comparator into [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p3.1.5 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} adds a comprehensive instruction mechanism that is crucial for identify clear directions to improve the agents, underlining comparator's key role in optimizing actor.
:::

<figure id="S5.T2" class="ltx_table">
<div id="S5.T2.3" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_center ltx_transformed_outer" style="width:433.6pt;height:181.4pt;vertical-align:-0.8pt;">
<span class="ltx_transformed_inner" style="transform:translate(-42.4pt,17.7pt) scale(0.836295598357327,0.836295598357327) ;"> </span>
<table id="S5.T2.3.1" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">
<tbody>
<tr id="S5.T2.3.1.1" class="ltx_tr">

<figure id="S5.F4" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/figs/curve_stark.png" id="S5.F4.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="598" height="143" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4: </span><span id="S5.F4.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Optimization dynamics of <span id="S5.F4.3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span> agents on <span id="S5.F4.3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">STaRK</span></span>. The figures show the validation performance (solid line) and their moving average (dash line) during the optimization of <span id="S5.F4.4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span>.</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S5.SS1.p4 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Takeaway 3: [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} effectively improves the agents during optimization]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Figure [[4]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 5.1 Textual and Relational Retrieval Tasks ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} illustrates the agents' performance on the validation set during optimization. Impressively, we found that [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} agents can significantly enhance performance, [e.g., ]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}improving from 35% to 75% on [Amazon]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} and from 20% to 78% on [MAG]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.5 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}. This evidence strongly supports the effectiveness of the instructions generated by our comparator. Moreover, our memory bank, which stores past best-performing actions, encourages [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p4.1.6 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} agents to gradually converge by the end of the optimization process.
:::

::: {#S5.SS1.p5 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Takeaway 4: [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p5.1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} can generalize to real-world tasks]{#S5.SS1.p5.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. Comparator suggests instructions tailored to a group of queries, which encourages agents to make more general modifications and generalize to novel queries. We validate this improved generalization capability by directly applying the optimized actions to the leave-out queries generated by humans from [STaRK ]{#S5.SS1.p5.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}benchmark. These human-generated queries exhibit a more distinct distribution than the question-answering pairs used to optimize the agents in our framework. We report the results in Table [[4]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} (Appendix [[B]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2 "Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) which shows that [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS1.p5.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} significantly outperforms other models, achieving an average improvement of 20.9% on Hit@1, demonstrating strong generalization across diverse human-generated queries.
:::
::::::::

:::::: {#S5.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [5.2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Image Retrieval Task {#image-retrieval-task .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S5.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
We further experiment on [Flickr30K Entities]{#S5.SS2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} \[[31](#bib.bib31){.ltx_ref}\], which focuses on image retrieval with 30k images representing the entity set. This dataset (Appendix [[A]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A1 "Appendix A Retrieval Tasks ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) enriches the entity information with pixel values and annotated bounding boxes along with their descriptive phrases. In Table [[2]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 5.1 Textual and Relational Retrieval Tasks ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS2.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} again presents significant improvements. Particularly, the Reflexion agents struggle with this task due to "overfitting" where they are easily misled by specific image data, leading to inappropriate actions. For example, they might attempt to "extract the color of a hat" from images that do not contain a hat. In contrast, [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS2.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} effectively avoids such pitfalls by employing the batch-wise contrastive reasoning that incorporates a more global perspective.
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p2 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Takeaway 5: [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS2.p2.1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} generates impressive and generalizable actions]{#S5.SS2.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. The final actions of the [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS2.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} agent achieve the advanced performance reported in Figure [[5]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 5.2 Image Retrieval Task ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} (left). We present these actions in Figure [[8]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Appendix C Prompts ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref} (Appendix [[B]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2 "Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}) due to space constraints. Notably, [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS2.p2.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} effectively handles the input query and impressively performs actions that leverage Inverse Document Frequency (IDF) scores to reweight phrase matching scores, ultimately synthesizing the final answers. Besides utilizing existing tools, [AvaTaR]{#S5.SS2.p2.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} agents can also develop high-level tools based on existing ones, such as the tool for reweighting phrase match scores by IDF scores. This capability suggests a future direction to maintain a dynamic tool library and generate instructions to enhance tool generation.
:::

<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/figs/curve_flickr.png" id="S5.F5.1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_landscape" width="598" height="449" alt="Refer to caption" />
</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>Performance (left) and <span id="S5.F5.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AvaTaR</span>’s optimization dynamics (right) on <span id="S5.F5.6.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps" style="font-size:90%;">Flickr30K-Entities<span id="S5.F5.6.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright">. </span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S5.F6" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/x4.png" id="S5.F6.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="461" height="124" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span><span id="S5.F6.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Representative instruction types from the comparator.</span> We provide three cases where the comparator guides the actor towards (1) better divide-and-conquer strategies for multi-step problem-solving, (2) more sensible differentiation between good and bad tool usage/combinations, and (3) adjustments in the weights to generate the final answers. We record the number of occurrences <span class="math inline"><em>X</em></span> under each instruction type over 25 iterations on <span id="S5.F6.8.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Flickr30K-Entities</span>, indicated by (<span class="math inline"><em>X</em></span>/25).</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S5.SS2.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Takeaway 6: Emerging Behaviors during Optimization]{#S5.SS2.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}. In Figure [[6]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.2 Image Retrieval Task ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, we present concrete cases illustrating key interactions between actor and comparator . In each instance, comparator identifies critical flaws, including information omission, ineffective tool usage, and suboptimal synthesis of varying scores. The instructions subsequently prompt actor to enhance retrieval strategies, tool selection, and precise score combinations. Furthermore, the frequent references to tool usage underscore comparator 's dedicated examination of tool utilization during optimization.
:::
::::::
::::::::::::::::::

:::: {#S6 .section .ltx_section}
## [6 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Conclusion {#conclusion .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S6.p1 .ltx_para}
In this study, we introduce [AvaTaR]{#S6.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}, a novel framework that automates the optimization of LLM agents for enhanced tool utilization in multi-step problems with a focus on complex retrieval tasks. [AvaTaR]{#S6.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} demonstrates remarkable improvements across four diverse datasets. This success can largely be attributed to the comparator module, which effectively refines agent performance through the iterative generation of holistic and strategic prompts. A key innovation of comparator is its use of contrastive reasoning with batch-wise sampling, enabling it to identify systemic flaws and extract robust \"gradients\" for comprehensive agent improvement across diverse scenarios. Future work can explore extending this methodology to other agent tasks and more dynamic environments.
:::
::::

::::::: {#A1 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix A ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Retrieval Tasks {#appendix-a-retrieval-tasks .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A1.p1 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[STaRK]{#A1.p1.8.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold .ltx_font_smallcaps}. On [STaRK ]{#A1.p1.8.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}benchmark, we are given a relation-text knowledge base, based on a knowledge graph $G = {(V,E)}$ and a collection of free text documents $D$. We represent the relation-text knowledge base of size n as $\mathcal{E} = {\{{(v_{i},d_{i},g_{i})}\}}_{i = 1}^{n}$, where $v_{i} \in V$ represent a node on the knowledge graph, $d_{i} \in D$ is the text documents related to the node, and $g_{i}$ is the connect component of $G$ where $v_{i}$ is in.
:::

::: {#A1.p2 .ltx_para}
The query set $Q$ provided in [STaRK]{#A1.p2.13.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps} is derived from both $G$ and $D$, where each $q_{i} \in Q$ contains requirements obtained from $d_{i}$ and $g_{i}$. And the answer set $A_{i}$, including $v_{i}$, is a set of nodes satisfying both relational and textual requirements. The task on STaRK can be defined as follows: Given the knowledge base $\mathcal{E}$ consisting of relational and textual information, and a text query $q_{i}$, the output is a set of nodes $A_{i}$ such that ${\forall a_{i}} \in A_{i}$, $a_{i}$ satisfied the relational requirements in knowledge graph and textual requirements in its text documents.
:::

::: {#A1.p3 .ltx_para .ltx_noindent}
[Flickr30K Entities]{#A1.p3.11.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold .ltx_font_smallcaps}. On Flickr30K Entities dataset, we are given a image-text knowledge base. We denote an image-text knowledge base of size $n$ as $\mathcal{E} = {\{{(v_{i},q_{i},T_{i})}\}}_{i = 1}^{n}$. Sample $i$ consists of an image $v_{i}$ and its corresponding descriptive caption $q_{i}$ along with the entity bounding box information $T_{i}$. Specifically, $T_{i} = {\{{(c_{i\hspace{0pt}j},p_{i\hspace{0pt}j})}\}}_{j = 1}^{b_{i}}$, where $b_{i}$ represent the number of bounding boxes annotated in image $i$, $c_{i\hspace{0pt}j}$ represents the coordinate of the j-th bounding box, and $p_{i\hspace{0pt}j}$ represents a set of text phrases that describe the entity in the corresponding bounding box.
:::

::: {#A1.p4 .ltx_para}
In our task, the image captions is served as the text query, therefore all the $q_{i}$'s in the dataset are not accessible to the agent in order to prevent information leakage. However, the agent could access $v_{i}$ and $T_{i}$ to fully utilize the vision and language information. The task on Flickr30K Entities can be defined as follows: Given the knowledge base $\mathcal{E}$ consisting of images with bounding boxes information, and a text query $q_{i}$, the output is an image $v_{i}$ such that it satisfied the visual requirements in image and textual requirements in the corresponding bounding boxes $T_{i}$
:::

<figure id="A1.F7" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2406.11200/assets/x5.png" id="A1.F7.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="461" height="156" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 7: </span><span id="A1.F7.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Example data on <span id="A1.F7.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Flickr30K Entities</span>.</span> Every entity is an image along with its included image patches and associated phrases with the image patches.</figcaption>
</figure>
:::::::

:::::: {#A2 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix B ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Additional Experimental Results {#appendix-b-additional-experimental-results .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A2.p1 .ltx_para}
In Table [[3]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, we provide the results on [STaRK ]{#A2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}using GPT-4 Turbo (0125) as the backbone LLMs.
:::

::: {#A2.p2 .ltx_para}
In Table [[4]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A2.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Appendix B Additional Experimental Results ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, we demonstrate [AvaTaR]{#A2.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_smallcaps}'s ability in generalizing to testing queries with different distributions than the question-answering pairs used to optimize the actor agents.
:::

::: {#A2.p3 .ltx_para}
In Figure [[8]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#A3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Appendix C Prompts ‣ AvaTaR: Optimizing LLM Agents for Tool-Assisted Knowledge Retrieval"){.ltx_ref}, we present the final actions which are made
:::

::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::: {#A3 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix C ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Prompts {#appendix-c-prompts .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A3.p1 .ltx_para}
We keep only two prompt templates for our framework on all tasks: (1) The prompt template given to actor as initially instructions, and (2) the prompt template given to the comparator to conduct contrastive reasoning and generate the instructions for the actor. Below are the complete templates:
:::

:::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::: {#A3.p2 .ltx_para}
This is the prompt given to actor as initially instructions:
